a

_. A draft. scheme has recently
been-put forward by the Minister
of Indusiry,"Chen Kung Po, of a
‘four-year industrial plan for,
Ching, an exposition of which

was given by the originator in a ,

recent issue of the Chinese
Economie Journal. Recognising
the very varied conditions that
exist. throughout China, it has
been thought advisable to restrict
the first acttvittes to the develop-
ment’of a central area.” That
chosen ns the most suitable, the
Yangtse Valley, which “ extends
vthrough six of the most fertile
“provinces, will bring within: reach
the purchasing powers of 300,-

000,000 people. It is thought that °

if key industries can be success-
fully founded ‘in four years in,
» this territory, the ‘basis will be
established for 1 truly united
China, The proposals naturally
cover agriculture and mining, as.
Well as general industry. , Some
of the industrial proposals are
of great interest. While there
Gre many collerles: in China,
2,000,000 tons of coal are imported
to ihe Yangtse Valley every year.
Tt ls believed that this is due to

the inefficiency of internal tran- ‘
" sport. To meet immediate needs,
. it ls proposed to establish a joint -

organisation to undertake the
Tepair of wagons. The facilitles
that such efforts, Properly con-
ducied, should produce, it ts
. thought, should ‘result in. the
Tesumption of full production by
ine, mines. and effective utilisa-
tion of capital and labour. An
{ron and steel works with a dally
Capacity of 500 tons was original-
ly contemplated. Instead of pre-
paring, in this single initial ‘step,
to supply what would amount to
one-quarter of the total’consump-
tlon of the country, it has been
decided to plan for half of_this

quantity. Iron ore ahd coal are

obtainable from mines to’ the
north and south of the river, but
difficulties in procuring sultable
.,C0al for coking. have not: been
entirely overcome. The coal flelds
have been: tested by drilling for
sultable’“fuel. A sulphate of
ammonia -plant “inyolving a capi-
tal expenditure of 17,000,000 dols,
"has been under consideration.
Another important ‘ttem in the
» Programme {s the founding of a

‘» ‘central machine works, For the

erection of ‘these, a plot-of land
has been purchased ‘by the Gov-
ernment from the MHstakuan
Municipality, but the agreement
of the banks in Shanghai to
finance the construction, with a
loan of 300,000 dols. was later

cancelled, owing to the financial :

,S'ringen¢gy caused by recent -hos-
"tities,

type of machinery to be produced
have not been completed. A
hydro-electric scheme’ on the
wUpper reaches of the Yangtse,
with a generating . capacity of:

200,000 K.W. has also’ .been con- °

templated,"but it was now regard-
ed as too expensive in first cost
to be considered in the present
financial cenditions.,/ The whole

Proposals, industrial, mining and’

agricultural, will involve the ex~
penditure.of 800,000,000 dols., dl-
vided: over four years, and’ tt 1s
" thought that {t will be ‘possible
to ralse this by the introduction
of State monopolies,

were completed.

cerns were to participate in the
merger, but owing to delay in

Seltetsu has. been postponed to
March. The new Company will
have aggregate assets of ¥345,-

| Reinforced ’ Concrete

a
a

Building Code

——

The Reinforced Concrete Struc-
tures Committee of the Bullding -.
Research Board last ‘week pub-'
lished’ their - recommendations’
for a code of practice for.the use
of reinforced. concrete in buil-
dings. The committee have

" given’ special consideration to the
: Stresses that should be permitted
for concrete. They have been’
impressed with» the great im-
‘provement in. cements and aggre-
gates, and have come to the
definite conclusion that’ industry
_ has at this time reached a: stage
“.In development when.. advantage
should be given to the engineer
who is prepared ‘to spend time
and money tn" producing .consis-''
tently tontrolled concrete. In
other words, the day has passed
when Gne stress anly should be
', Permitted for a mix regardless
of the “care exercised and
Seneral level of strength achiev-
‘ed. With this in mind the com-
mittee have - proposed : three
. Srades of concrete. and é
Ordinary Grade © |
The fist grade,’ designated
“ordinary” ‘grade, is intended for
use in cases where, rigid control:
1s not exercised’ and whére con’
,Siderations of economy or con-
,Venlence are such that it’ be-
comes impracticable to’ insist on”
tests. The stregses are regarded
as conservative and representa-
‘tive of present practices: under
such conditions. Thé second, or
» “high,” grade calls fot . preli-
minary tests, for greater control
during the programme of tests
on the deposited concrete. The.
Stresses for this class of concrete
Tepresent present practice. in
certain works in” this country,
‘and the committee are. satisfied
that no. difficulty .{s likely to
arise in producing the strengths
demanded. The supper Mmit of
the "stresses approximates to
that allowed by the’ Ministry of
Transport for use‘ in ‘bridges.’
, The third, or “ special,” grade
calls for an even greater, exer-
clse of. control and of testing.
For this grade‘ thé: procedure,
although common abroad. Is’ new
to practice .in this country in
that ‘the actual: predetermined
, Strength of. the’ concrete’ forms *
the, basis for, “the permissible
Stresses. While the ‘tatlos be-
tween the works test .require-
ments’ and the permissible stresses
-have been maintained at the
same values as for the other
grades, 2 wider margin-has been
allowed between the predeter-
mined strength and the works
Strength in order to allow for
any factors’ still: uncontrolled.
In the case of columns where
bending is important the Jncrease
in permissible’. stresses ..is. how~
“ever, more apparent than real,.
since the ‘methods of calculation
involve allowance for the bending||
whereas in the past such allowan-
ces were not normally required.

Steel. and Permissible Stresses
The comniittee. consider that
stresses in steel should be limited
"by two factors—the factor ‘of
safety-on the yleld point of the:
steel and the extent of the crack-,,
. ing that is likely to occur on the
tension side of a member th
‘bending. In the opinion of the-
committee, an adequate factor
“of safety will, be assured by
making the permissible stress
approximately 0.45 of the yleld~
point. stress. It. {s “considered °
that ordinary mild steel to. Bri-
tish. Standard Specification No.
15 may be. safely stressed ‘to
18,000 lb. per square inch.’ The

!

extent of the cracking ‘should in: -
general not go beyond that ex- «

Perlenced with, a steel stress, of:
' of 20.000 Ib. per square inch, and
it has therefore been ‘ deemed
consistent to specify a yield-
_point requirement of 44,000 ‘Ib.

butcher and had beaten him down -

trom 1s, ‘3d. to 10d. for the piece

of meat she fancied for the week- ,

Commonwealth

The members of the expedition are
the Rev. H. E. Warren’ and the Rev,
A, J, Dyer, both poasesding con-

siderable knowledge and eperiane .

ce of the North Australian blacks,
and Mr. John Porter, wireless oper-
ator. They have gone from Thurs-
day Island to the Groote Bylandt
Mission, and then will travel to the
headquarters of the Balamumu tribe.
The tribal belief is that a white
man, or mea, ‘will come to- avenge
the death of Constable’MeColl, They
await the avenging party. , :
Few examples of rare daring and
cuurage in the ‘annals of Christian
missions excel this Australian ex-
pedition. Meésrs. Warren and Dyer
will go, absolutely unarmed, into
the thidst of the disturbed Balam
umu tribe. “
“Tg ig surprising how easy it is
to chango these, simple children |
from their purpose by appeiling to
their sense of humour,” Mr, Dyer
stated in”an interview before his

»| departure, writes 2 Sydney corres=

I propoge.to play to the.accompant-
ment of comic grins and gestures—
what you would call ‘acting the
goat.’ Onco T D
their hostility will, disappear.

“Den Quixote” which opens at"
the Central Theatre today is the
picture, made Jast autumn in the
South) of France with Chaliapine in
the title rolé and George Robey, as:
Sancho Panza.

Strictly .speaking this is
“Tvan “tho Terrible’ morc than
twenty “ypats ‘ago—but he js, per-
haps, the most, important capture
the films have" tnade’ since talkies

-arrived. Feodor Ivanovitch . Cha-
liapine, a native

of Kazan, had
been a, shoemaker’s apprentice, .
railway porter and stevedore before
he made his operatic debut. in
1892; since then he has progressed
to"intemational renown and has
toured all the principal cities of
the world as an eperatie star, The «
film is not, in fact, based “on that
opera but is taken from Cervantes’
classic story.by Paul Morand the
French writer. Special music has

been written for Chaliapine’s three

songs.

The English treatment is the ‘work
«t.John Farrow, the’ English scen-
arist ship Hollywood. In the im-
portant role of Sancho Panza, Don
Quixote’s henchman,- George Robey
makes an oui ‘anticipated ap-
pearance: The character is almost,
as important as that of Quixote’s
and the English stage and screen

A fleet of twenty-three airplanes,

thrilling aerial combat sequences
of “Acy of Aves”, Richard Dix’s
new starring ., picturo for RWKO-
Radio ‘Pictures showing to-day at
the King’s Theatre ‘with Elizabeth
Allan.

The"planes used in the picture

ure Spudg and Fokkers, Nieuports |”
and Camels. Many of the ships
werd rented from Garland Lincoln,
famous air dore-devil, who leads a
group of twelve stunt flyers through
the dangerous air manoeuvres which
highlight tho film:
. In “Ace of Aces Dix is seen
as a war pilot who js finally ap-.
palled at the thought of killing his
fellow men. Able support is given
the star. by Elizabeth. "allan, Kalph |
Bellamy, Theodore Newton, Frank
Conroy ‘and Art Jarrett. J. Walter

dity should prove his strong point,

noiable pictures.. Sidney came spe- |
cially to Europe to play as'Maria'!
and while here, appeared alsa op-
posite Emil “Jannings in “The Mer-

, One of the Witnesses called yes-

geant Najor in.the Police Force,

ture of the pure article and what
is known as vegetable ghee.

Mr. Whyte Smith quoted many
authorities who described ghee as
clarified butter, and Mr. Omar el.
Arculli, manager of Messrs. A. F.
Arculll & Sons, Army Contractors,
who was called’ as an expert wit-
hess, substantiated ‘this. .

An Important Case,

Outlining the case for the pro-
Secution, Mr. Whyte Smith, said
that the case was that what’ they

|| got. was not ghee so the definition

of the word “ghee” is, accordingly
Important, Counsel continued
that he would like to submit that
the. ‘case .jis an important
case in view of the large
Indian" community here and. in
view of the fact that ghee is an
important part of their diet.. His
submission was, that’ ghee . is
clarified"butter and nothing else.
. Continuing, Mr. Whyte - Smith
said, “Just as we can,go and’ buy
butter or margarine, so can an
Indian go and buy ghee and a
vegetablé substitute ,or any other
substitute. When: you come 'to
consider the relative prices of
ghee and this substitute, the
-question of the purchaser getting
what he asks for is very important.
Ghee {s about 80 cents per lb. and
the vegetable substitute ts. only

about 2 quarter of its price.. I

don’t want. your: Worship to go
into the subject of food ‘qualities
or vitamins, but I think It'is quite
obvious from a’ public health point
of view that a good deal of harm,
may be caused if poeple are buy-
ing this vegetable substitute think-
ing it is ghee, and if they ‘still
think it is ghee in spite of its
low price, then-I submit fhey are
being defrauded. «

Mr. Whyte Smith then went on
to quote several authorities on
what {5 ghee after which he told
the . Court that on the:day in
question Inspector Lamprill: went

for half a pound of ghee. A foki
got the article out from a tin
marked “ghee”, and charged +35
cents for It.,

The officer. paid. for the ghee |

and then informed the foki that
he. was going to have the thing
analysed. » Following the’ usual
procedure. he divided the ghee
into three lots and put them each

into, a separate bottle.

Expert Evidence

‘was clarified butter, Witness. went

‘SHORT SCHOOL YEAR: REVIEWED

© :

The Headmaster said In part:—

The .maximurh enrolment _ for
the year was 626, the maximum
number on the roll at any one
time 600 and the average attend-
ance 576, “Our accommodation is

, strained to the utmost: capacity.

» tion in’ English and moreover I am] nicay

I think that more boys are now.
completing the [full school course |
than formerly, Le going right
| through" from Class 8 to Class ‘1:
this is probably due to’ the reall-
sation by parents that a .boy, Is
better equipped for the battle of
fe these days - by leaving: him
longer at school, and also to the
polley. of admitting beginners to”
-Claks 8 at an earlier age than for- i
merly. ‘The Government age limit.
to Class 8 1s 14 (13 English reckons
ing)—we get many boys now ab
11 (10 English). .I think the mit
in thig respect has been reached—
I do not think it is’ wise to take
*them younger: they do not appear
to respond very well to an educa-

informed that their Chinese Js
weak ‘at 10. . It would be on. ad-
vantage if all but the brightest
boys remained for 2 years in Class,

subjects which from, a cultura.
point of view are much more im-
portant for, school boys and which
from an examination point 0.
view are much more difficult. One
rarely hears of a distinction jn
any of these: the old Matricula-
tion syllabuses were very wide:
We were prepared to pit all om
Class 1 boys in for 9 ‘proposed de-
partmental .examination in ."the
coming June on the. lines of the
1935 School Certificate. Examina-
‘tion and I regret that the Examin-
ation had to be .abandoned.

General Education

A general education will always
be more {friutful than a specialis-
ed One - in’ developing initiative,
adaptability and self-reliance. At
the same time !t is impossible not
to recognize that many boys" now
In the varlous schools are not cap-
able of pursuing a purely second-.
ary, education, that the ‘wastage

and again jn 1930. I will quote

: «Staff ;

year! Mr, Kay ‘took my place. Mr.

and act as trainer. This would
be of great benefit to ‘the boys
and to the ‘organisation, of games.

Discipline |. ;
The discipline and tone of the
school are good: I should like,
however, to see the Prefects exert,
their authority a little, more ,; in
many minor waysi,: They rather

"shrink from undertaking . respon-
, sibility. I have always found Chi-

- tion results. Perhaps. I

nese boys very amenable to dis-

exercise of authority by their fellow
students. It 1s an fnteresting sub-'

ment. The Prefect system in day
achools does not as 3 tule work

7 Examinations

‘.* There was no Junior Local Ex-
“amination in 1933—the next Uni-
versity Examination will. be the.
School Certificate ,Examination in
June 1935. In : Matriculatfon,, 20,
passed out of 54, and 4 gained the
Senior Lotal We congratulate Li
Shi Yi on gaining honours, with
distinctions in Mathematics. and
Chinese-he has since gone'to the,
University with’ an. educational
scholarship. . It was. apparently a
Jean year as: regards: Matricula-
should.
explain. that, for the last 3 or 4
-years,. we have been anticipating
the conditions of the new Schoo?
Certificate Examination and here
‘been gradually working up to. the
vosition whereby all boys in‘ the
‘Upper School (Classes 3-1) are ob-
ged to "take all the 7 subjects
_ in the.5 groups. We have now
‘penalized that. Our Matriculation
candidates last, year (as also. our
Junior: Tocal candidates $n 1932).
‘were not encouraged or even al-
“lowed to concentrate on such “soft
“examination options’ as" Mechan~
es, Drawing,, Mandarin, Co-ordin-
_ ate Geometry, etc., or. .to-substituts

-graphy, ‘Physics. and Chemistry—

educationally "unsound. These boys
would probably find their.level in
a different type of school, prob-
ably vocational.” Further ex-!
perience has not changed. my
views expressed four yéars ago.
At home these boys would be
drafted at the age 11 (correspond-
ing roughly ‘is class 4, in Hong
Kong) to Modern, Central, Trade’
or. Junior Technical Schools—
schools with an industrial 01
commercial bias and whict  -give,
I believe, a ‘practical training tc
a greater or lesser “degree with-
out being, purely vocational, Such
types of schools should undoub-
tedly form a part of the educa-

tlonal organisation of Hong Kong,

ithe existing facilities, being giv-
pn of course to boys of real pro-
mise to take the full secondary
course up to the School Certifi-’
cate, and to enter the University.

fe A Suggestion

civrical statf only trom those whe
Nave passea the School Certificatz
Exammation, Le., substitute" this
examination for the special 0.40.
Examination’ which js “now held

mentary by ‘comparison... It is
now taken. by pDoys from ail
classes. Boys who have - stayed
the full course at school should
have, I think, a prior claim on

product. The "Hongkong Cham-
}bers of” Commerce could later on
be similarly approached for the
recruitment of office staffs;. in
fact an employment agency for
youths could well be set up undes
the auspices of thei Education De-
partment. All this would streng-
then’ the School: Certificate Ex+
amination enormously, and would,
I think, be of great benefit to
the Colony generally. ‘

to weary you with an educational
Speech. This ought to be an_un-
spoilt day for you ydungsters who
nave ao glorious vista of holidays
ahead. I congratulate the school

shali do well to remember how
much,.of a school’s success is due

staff, (Applause).

Take More to Cricket

The school seems to have dine
well on the Sporting side. I am
glad to. say that it usually does.
I only wish that more of you
would take to cricket, one of the
three best games in the world.
Qc. and the Chinese Recreation
Club stand ina close Telation one
ito the other, and it ls my, strong
personnal hope that if I come back
here on a visit In two or three
years time, I shall find the CRC.
Cricket "team once more going
strong as in. the palmy days that
many of us remember with affec-
tion.

Well,.I mustn’t detain you un-
duly. .There is just: one” ‘other
point mentioned in the Head-
master’s Report that I. shoulg
ike to touch on, that is the best
age for Chinese boys to' begin here
at the bottom of the: school. It
1s absolutely essential that they
should have @ really good founda-
tion In the reading and writing
of Chinese, thelr own language.
before they come here. I agree
that 14 (Chinese reckoning) is toc
old, and that Chinese eleven 1s
in miany cases probably too young.
If you split the difference. me)
opght to work out “about right,
and that would enable a boy te
reach the top of the School a‘
the age of 19 English reckoning.

‘Well, that is really all, and.1
thank you, Mr. de Rome, very

warmly for your kind reference te

myself and for ‘asking me_ heré

‘to-day. I haope it will notvbe toc

‘poys at the University and.

‘great a strain,on your forbear-
ance ‘if I ask you in your discre-
tion, to give the'"school a holiday
at some suttable date on my ac-
count. (Applause),

MISS ATKINS’ REPORT

Miss Atkins report was as
follows:— '

"Mrs. Borrett, Bishop Hall Ladies | (Licentiate) of the
and Gentlemen:—The year 1033 )

was on’ the whole very uneventful.
Steady work in classroom and
playground with little to interrupt

the daily routine may sound rather

monotonous, but school life is a
life of variety and monatony. has |
very Httle chance to take root.
The system of house prefects in-
stituted early in the year has
abundantly” justified ‘Itself; the
prefects, have taken their duties
seriously and have done their work"
well. |,
‘An experiment was made in the
summer to substitute early morn-
ing for afternoon school. It was
found unsatisfactory’ for.
reasons, one that transport diffi-
culties “made it impossible for
‘everyone to arrive in ‘time for
school, and secondly,—and: this
seemed to the Staff to be most im-
portant.—it killed all social life.
The tiffin hour ceased tobe a time
of friendly . intercourse, the Wa
Kwong Club could no longer or-
ganise parties and meetings after
school hours, and one was too tired
after the ‘long morning even to
enjoy bathing picnics. So ‘after a
month's trial we went back to the
éld hours. - : ‘
Apart from this month, the Wa
Kwong ‘and Rainbow .Clubs have
functioned well. In May we took
part in a Singing and Dramatic
‘Competition. between various
schools. It was held in St. Paul's
Girls’ College, and St.” Stephen's
carried off the prize ‘for singing.

fh October we held a Exhibition |

of Chinese arts .and industries,
various groups of girls competing,

two |}

raga is to be congratulated on

3
\ having won the Teacher's Diploma

Trinity ‘College
of Music, London,” and Choy O!
Chee on having passed, the ‘prac-
tical part of the examination for
the Teacher's Diploma (Associate).
Five old girls. are ‘on our own
teaching staff. Five this year have
entered the Hong Kong Untfversity,

| two the Yenching University, Pel-

ping, and mahy others are doing
useful -work in ‘schools, hospitals
and: offices. They have our. in-

terest and good wishes, and we are [

helped by their loyalty ‘and love. te

‘And so, with hearts filled with
thanksgiving; we face the New,
Year with: coursge and falth,
trusting that God, Who’ has led
us in the past, will guide us all
the days. (Applause.)

pealed to us... : :

Fall, itself -his gift. In the sum-

MRS. BORRETT’S APPEAL
Addressing the school after the

‘prize distribution, Mrs. Borrett:
congratulated ,the ‘staf! and stud-

ents upon their. success and ap-
pealed to. the girls to remember
that their teachers had once been

students themselves. If the stud-
ents should feel a little rebellious

at times, they would do well to try
and see both sides of the picture,

and. ‘remember that while -the

teacher ts doing her’ best for the
pupil, she also had to look after

the discipline and the traditions’ of

the school.

THE ‘PARLIAMENT: OF MEN,

Now that the League of Nations

J ds once more under discussion and

looks ° as if. it has escaped ‘from
{ts fate of moribundity, lt might
be as well if we considered the
Possibility of the World Economic
Conference in its relationship to
@ settling of the economic ques-
tions 'which are so closely related’

‘| to” political matters. ig

The Parliament of Man, 2
phrase coined by Lord Tennyson,
once, Poet Laureate; ‘most aptly
described the World "Economic
Conference, which was opened in
London “by the King of England
jast sumier. There were present
168 ‘delegates representing 66
states, of which’ 56-are members:
of’the League of Nations. They
included. one .President, . eleven
Prime Ministers, . twenty - Foreign
Ministers; about the same number.
of Finance. Ministers, and’ some
twenty-five ° holders of. other

CANTON |

: Stabilisation of Bank

Notes

(From Our Special Correspondent)

i Canton, February 3.

While officials 'of the Kwangtuag
Provincial Bank and of the Pro-
vincial Department of Finance’ are
deliberating ways ahd means ‘to
Stabilise. the’ government bank-
notes, they suffered a further drop
to-day and had a tendency of
going further downward.

Provincial banknotes of $5 and
$1 denominations are. worth 93
while notes -of
the Canton Municipal Rank are
valued 85 cents per dollar. Fitc-
tuation continued this. afternoon,
and there is-no hope for recovery
to par value for sometine, at
least until after the Chinese New
Year,

Treasury officials pointed out
that there should be no effect on
the provincial banknotes by the
influx: of notes unsealed and cir-
culated by landlords, as so far
only, “about $30,000 were unsealed.
In any case, speculators: used this
8s ‘2M. opportunity te. push down
the value of the notes and Feap
a windfall
» .Im.view of the demand of mer-
chants to-make.payments i silver
colns, the Kwangtung  Proviricial
Government has issued ath’ order,

at any" payment exceeding one
dollar shall ‘be made In banknotes.

‘| Tax’ bureaus and schools are re-

buked for insisting én collecting
silver coins, and ‘punishment will
be meted‘ out to those who refuse
to accept banknotes of, the Kwang-
tung Provincial Bank. “

Indicating their. co-operation
with. the government, the leading
Chinese banks are willing to ac-
cept banknotes 2s’ deposits and
will repay ‘depositors in silver
coins whenever they. are available:
However, some of them have re-
celved: enough banknotes" to ‘the
prescribed amount and are unable
-to accept further deposits in paper
monéy.

Sunlor government officials, ate
hard hit, ‘by the depreciation, - as
they are paid.in banknotes,” On:
the: other hand, many shops and
‘firms. pay, their staf! in - silver’
edins, so, that they may not suffer
any loss. by depreciation...

NO EXPORT
_ TARIFFS

Decision of of the South-
West Political Council

(From Our Special Correspondent)

—_—-

~ Canton, February 3."

Following the decision of the
South-west. Political Council to
‘abolish export-tariff and inter-
Port transit dues concerning
native goods, over forty guilds
have petitioned .Mr. Ou’Fang Pu,
the Commissioner of National Re-

venues, that the date of exemp- |

= be commenced on. February
5.

Having ‘passed the foregoing. ‘ce-
Solution on February 6 at the
motion of Mr. Tang Shao Yi, the
South-west Political Council ‘arder-
ed, Mr. Ou Fang Pu to enforce this"
decree and to add necessary de-
tailed: regulations, In taking this
Sweeping measure, the South-west
Political Council is’ prompted by
the ‘necessity,’ ‘of promoting export,
trade, since’ few countries in the
world levy export duties, " °

“When. this order . is put into
effect, exporters in Kwangtung
will have an. advantage over their |
colleagues in other ports‘of China, |

-| where export tariff duty is payable.

The question of tariff is withizi the.
jurisdiction’ of the’ Ministry of
‘Finance, but the South-west’ Poli-
tical Council seems to have un-
Mimited power over matters in this.
Province and* is able to ‘regulate
even customs administration. ~

Silk merchants are particulary
pleased ‘by the abolition of the
export duty, because they have to
pay very high duty in the United’
States which Imports considerable
Chinese silk. Without Chinese
tariff barrier, silk exports" will be
able to sell more goods in: America:
where ‘the demand for this com-
modtty has. somewhat improved.

| WORK OF: CRIMINAL GANGS"

. Paris, February 8. '

The organised plundering’ and
bloodshed of" Wednesday night’s
disorders is unanimously . deno-
minated by the Press here on
Thursday as “no political demon-
strations but work' of organised
criminal gangs.’ Countless: shops
were broken Into and looted on
the Champs, Elysee, the’ Rue
Tronchet and the fashionable Rue
Rivoli, the police being met with
pistol fire on their mere appearance
on. the scene of the mass robberies
in most cases. The Capital there-

-| fore Is breathing a double. sign

of relief on Thurday as Doumergue
takes the firm hold on the reins.—

few disturbances, stones were

sung. The Pollce,. however, were

HANDLEY PAGE
INVENTION |

France To Use Slotted
"Plane Wing ;

q » London, Februnry 8,

‘The ‘french Alr Ministry have
concluded an agreement with
Messrs. Handley Page Ltd. and
have pald a substantial sum to
them for the full. rights of the
French Air" Force and French
manufacturers to fix the Handley
Page slotted wings, to their air-
craft in French territory.

This decision to purchase by

the French Government was taken
after exhaustive trials which fully
substantiated the increased safety
which the slotted wing givés,

Incidentally they confirmed the
wisdom of, the decision of the Bri-

tish Air Ministry if standardising |

the fitting the Royal Alr Force
craft, the right té do so being

acquired a few years ago for
£100,000 *

The slotted wing device consists
mainly In auxiliary winglets which
in normal flight lie flat against
the leading or front edge of the
wings. When an. earoplane loses
flying speed and gets, into 2 poten-
tlally dangerous attitude, :
nose upand tail down,

from the“front of the wings and

the effect, of the slot thus created:
between the winglets and. the.

main plane Js to preserve an un-
broken air flow over the structure.

The pilot thus maintains full
control in circumstances which

might otherwise send him into a}
nosé-dive and spin, 4 state of.

affairs responsible for 90 per cent.
“pot, fatal flying accidents. —British,
, Wireless Service. 7 +

‘LAISSEZ,

. ‘Washington, February. a.
_ The" Senate passed o Bill ap-
propriating $950,000,000 for unem-
ployment, relief ‘which was Passed

5 votes to 2.
A bloodless revolution .has oc-

curred, declared Mr. Ickes, Secre-.

tary for the Interior, In a speech
describing the accession to power
of President Roosevelt. The good
old days of Laissez Faire ore gone
for ever, and the United States is
at the dawn of a new era. He
predicted that’ all social -abuses
and injustices would be wiped out,
sweat. shops eliminated, slums
cleared,. and unemployment in-
surance and old age pensions will
soon be introduced, giving a, feel-
‘ing of security to the workless and
aged.—Reuter.

, Shanghai, - February, .

Pearl Buck, sailed this morn
‘by the Conte Rosso on, an exten
ed tour through’ "Indo-Ching:
Siam, the Malay States and India,
‘Yn search of material for articles:
and books.—Reuter. .

American missionary in China,
was herself: engaged for some
years in missionary work and firs¢!

‘ings on sociological subjects. Her;

| novels, all dealing .with the lives:
‘}of. ordinary’ Chinese folk have:

}of the writers of to-day. - :

Others ‘Activities

Our other. . activities need. be

free’ night school, a really in-
stitution, has now been in ex-
istence for more than twenty

mentary education in both Chinese
and'.English to. those -whose eir-
cumstances do. not permit of their

in | attending any.of the day-schools.

The'teaching staff consists of thir-
'ty-six, each of whom gives one
evening in the'week, and there has
never been 2 “shortage. ‘of volun-
teers. A’ number of: others from
the College are assisting regularly

In eye-sight,,.as he himself was

certain amount of recreation.”

bles ghee and ‘which ,was made

Mr. Whyte

call’ the genuine article?—Just

: THE “BIG STICK”
i,- Mv. Shaw charactorized the
-Merchant Marine policy of the
| United States -as a “big. stick’:
| American shipowners earnestly. deny
that there is any dig stick involved.
Tf we were carrying as much of
| Great Britain's commerce ‘ag . she
i herself curries, if we, were carrying
ithe bulk of her passengers, if. we
‘running holiday cruises.out of her
‘ports and demanding a still greater
; share of her business, ‘then we, might
| justly be accused of wielding a big

stick. ,We give two-thirds’ of our }

freight’ to foreign ships and about
three-fourths of our pnssengets..
Any greater percentage would mean
the extinction of American lines in
foreign trade, 7
q ‘The -principle, of » Government
. support for shipping, as provide in
{the Jones-White. Act of 1928, seems
+to anger our foreign friends most’

‘of all. All of the woes of world ship--}

ping, are laid-at our doorstep, Our
' policy ig variously described as un-
fair, unethical, unfriedly. British
shipowners “know: full Well that,
without some form' of Government
: aid, American’ vessels cannot possib-
ily compete. with the more cheaply
operated vessels of Foreign, Powers,
Either we have somt measure of
support to offset the higher coat of
operating, under the American flag,
or we have no™ ships in foreign
trade. .

SUBSIDIZING OF SHIPS
“One wonders.why, if stbsidies
jaié.su unfair, they are now urged

they are a reply té tho ‘subsides of

| Greag Britain has-not, hesitated to

; The Busitania and Mauretania were
| built with Government loans ....
; Thiy was at a, time when the Amer-
ican Merchant Marine was practic-
ally nonexistent. Practically all
nations to-day subsidize shipping
in some form or other, despite the
fact that they aro already subsidiz-
ed by lower-operating costa. ‘The
British mercaitile,marine, as an ine
stance, employs 43,000 Lascar sea-
men, at wages ranging down to $7
® month‘ .

London, February 8. ,

Len: Harvey put up one of the
greatest “fights of his life at
Alpert Hall to-night . when
against. all predictions of the ex-
perts, he defeated Larry Gains,
holder of the Empire heavy-
welght title. “ot

By his successful challenge... Len
Harvey gained his fourth cham-
Pionship of the year, his previous
outstanding performance — being
Jack

Nt

ry

his ‘smart’ victory” over’

Petcrsen, for the British title, .
» Harvey won to-night’ on points |
after fifteen rounds contest
with. the Canadian champion,
‘which was packed through with
thrills. ” .o4

eo

Weight Disparity

Although''he was at a serious |.
{disadvantage in wetght” (Larry
Gains welghed in ‘at 15 st. 54 lbs.
2nd, Harvey . at 12 ‘st. 10 Ibs)
Harvey boxed brilliantly to secure
an ‘unexpected victory by a wide
margin of points.

Gains perhaps ‘had only himseli
to“blame, He adopted surprising
tactics, allowing Harvey to take
the ‘initiative and to do the for-
cing, instead of making the best
use of hts weight supertority by
going all out from the. first bell.

COURTAULD'S

©

.  « DLendon, January 11.
Courtaulds, Ltd, the £34,000,000

artificial slik manufacturing con-,
| At the last annual meeting of the| .

cern, made an important an-
nouncement yesterday regarding |
its future policy.

Production is to be extended by
over 30 per cent. and

Reductions, averaging about 3d
Per Ib are to te: made in the com-
pany’s Viscose yarn prices.

The cut in prices has been ren-
dered possible by. the increase in:
‘production and by recently éffect-.
ed.economies. .

A few weeks ‘ago Courtaulds an-~
nouneed a cut in. the prices of cer-

tain.of its leading acetate products..

The price reductions and the high-

er output will place the company in ;

a stronger position to meet Japan-

ese competition abroad. It is.

pointed out in Manchester, how-
ever, that Japanese prices will still
be considerably - oki those of
Britain...

Courtaulds has a substantial in-
terest in American ..Viscose Cor-

poration, one of the biggest Ameri- |
can producers, and !t is also link- H

ed up on the Continent with Snia
Viscosa, the German Glanzstof?:
Co., and A.K.U, °

RIVALRY: WITH JAPAN
‘Manchester, January 17.

The announcément by Courtaulds
was greeted as good news in Man-
chester trade’ circles to-night.
The increased output Wwill:.. be
doubly welcomed by those Lancs
manufacturers’ who,” during the
t middle“of Jdst summer, experienced
considerable difficulty in “obtaining
adequate supplies ‘of yarn.

Sir Ernest Thompson, chairman
of the rayon section of the Man-
chester Chamber... of.” Commerce
sommented: -

“Japan has already indicated
that she intends to increase her
production for 1934 very consider-!

ably—far. more than .30 per cent..

—and so one presumes that
.| Courtaulds are. preparing to coun-
ter the. increased Japanese output.

“The reduction of "3d a pound

‘Twill not bring ‘our prices near. the

Japanese, but. there is a-keen fight
between ourselves and the Japan-
ese in the rayon trade. The price}.

the increased supplies ; will help - us

‘Leven more.”

Rayon production ‘in’ Great ‘prt
tain has risen’ meee In Novem~'

. (Special Alr-Mail Service)

ber last, it reached the ‘record
total of 8,550,0001b. » The nionthly
average in 1924 was only. 2,110,0001b.

company Mr. Samuel. Courtauld,
the. chairman, said that the Japan-
j ese ‘policy of undercutting prices
made it very. difficult to uphold
agreements among European pro-
ducers. It was impossible for a
British manufacturer to compete in
an open market’ with stich:a com-
{ petitor, he added, and no. general
| taxis could Tedress the balance.
